Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, thank you. Um… honestly, the thing that finally made me come in is something that sounds kind of small, but it’s been happening enough that I’m starting to feel embarrassed by it. I work in advertising sales, and we have these long sales strategy meetings where everyone is expected to sit and focus, and I just… keep getting up.

Like, I know I’m supposed to stay seated. I’m not confused about that. But after a while I’ll find some reason to stand up, like refilling my water even if I don’t really need more, or walking over near the printer, or just sort of pacing for a minute like I’m checking something. I try to make it look purposeful, but really I just feel like I can’t keep myself in the chair for the whole meeting.

It’s not causing a huge crisis or anything, but it’s noticeable to me, and I worry it’s noticeable to other people too. That’s basically what pushed me to come talk to someone.

Yeah, the main place I really notice it is those long sales strategy meetings at work. That’s the example that kind of made me think, “Okay, this is becoming a thing.” I’ll be sitting there knowing I’m expected to just stay in my seat and listen or contribute like everyone else, and after a while I start looking for a reason to get up.

It’s not always dramatic or anything. I’ll refill my water even if I barely drank any, or I’ll go stand near the printer like I’m checking something, or I’ll pace a little bit and try to make it seem like I’m being productive. But honestly, it’s more that I feel like I can’t just stay seated through the whole thing. I’m aware of it while it’s happening, which makes it a little embarrassing.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi, thank you. Um… honestly, the thing that finally made me come in is something that sounds kind of small, but it’s been happening enough that I’m starting to feel embarrassed by it. I work in advertising sales, and we have these long sales strategy meetings where everyone is expected to sit and focus, and I just… keep getting up.\n\nLike, I know I’m supposed to stay seated. I’m not confused about that. That’s the example that kind of made me think, “Okay, this is becoming a thing.” I’ll be sitting there knowing I’m expected to just stay in my seat and listen or contribute like everyone else, and after a while I start looking for a reason to get up.\n\nIt’s not always dramatic or anything. I’ll refill my water even if I barely drank any, or I’ll go stand near the printer like I’m checking something, or I’ll pace a little bit and try to make it seem like I’m being productive. But honestly, it’s more that I feel like I can’t just stay seated through the whole thing. I’m aware of it while it’s happening, which makes it a little embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you also feel restless or find yourself getting up in other situations where you’re expected to stay seated, like during meals with family or while watching a movie at home?\n\nPatient: I mean… the clearest example is still work, honestly. It’s not like I’m storming out of the room or anything, but I do feel like I can’t just stay in the chair the whole time.\n\nWith things like meals or movies, I’m not sure I’ve paid as much attention to it. The work meetings are what made me think, “Okay, this is noticeable enough that maybe I should talk to someone.”\n\n[Last Question]\nOutside of work meetings, do you ever feel restless or have the urge to get up during other situations like meals with friends or family, or while watching a movie at home?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Honestly, outside of work I’m not as sure. I don’t want to say it never happens, but the thing that really stands out—and the reason I came in—is the work meetings. Those long sales strategy meetings are where I notice it the most because there’s this clear expectation that I’m supposed to stay seated and focused, and I just keep finding reasons not to be in the chair.

Like I’ll get up to refill my water when I don’t really need any, or I’ll walk over toward the printer and kind of stand there like I’m checking on something, or I’ll pace for a minute and try to make it look like it has a purpose. It’s not like I’m trying to be disruptive. I just get this feeling like I can’t sit through the whole thing without moving. With meals or movies, I guess I haven’t paid as close attention, but the meetings are definitely the situation where it’s become obvious to me.

Those long sales strategy meetings are where I notice it the most because there’s this clear expectation that I’m supposed to stay seated and focused, and I just keep finding reasons not to be in the chair.\n\nLike I’ll get up to refill my water when I don’t really need any, or I’ll walk over toward the printer and kind of stand there like I’m checking on something, or I’ll pace for a minute and try to make it look like it has a purpose. It’s not like I’m trying to be disruptive. I just get this feeling like I can’t sit through the whole thing without moving. With meals or movies, I guess I haven’t paid as close attention, but the meetings are definitely the situation where it’s become obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any times outside of work—like when you’re at home watching TV or having a meal with friends or family—when you’ve felt restless or had the urge to get up and move around?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think, and honestly I don’t have a great outside-of-work example that comes to mind. I know you’re asking specifically about home or meals or things like that, and I’m still not coming up with a really solid example there. The thing that’s clearest for me is still work, honestly. What made me come in is that during those long sales strategy meetings, when I know I’m supposed to stay seated like everyone else, I keep getting up anyway.

Sometimes I’ll tell myself I’m just refilling my water, but I don’t even really need more water. Or I’ll go stand near the printer and kind of pace around like I’m checking on something, when really I think I just need to not be sitting in the chair anymore. It feels like this internal pressure to move, but I try to make it look normal because I don’t want people to think I’m being rude or not paying attention.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I know you’re asking about home or social stuff, and I feel like I’m not giving you a great answer there because I can’t pull up one clear example like, “Oh, at dinner last Friday I couldn’t sit still.” It’s more that the strongest, most obvious version of it has been at work.

What made me come in, honestly, is that during those long sales strategy meetings, I keep getting up even though I know I’m supposed to stay seated. Like I’ll decide I need to refill my water, even if I barely drank any, or I’ll drift over toward the printer and sort of pace or stand there like I’m checking on something. It’s not like I’m running around the room, but I feel this urge to get out of the chair, and then I try to make it look like I have a normal reason so it doesn’t seem rude.

That’s the example that kind of made me think, “Okay, this is becoming a thing.” I’ll be sitting there knowing I’m expected to just stay in my seat and listen or contribute like everyone else, and after a while I start looking for a reason to get up.\n\nIt’s not always dramatic or anything. I’ll refill my water even if I barely drank any, or I’ll go stand near the printer like I’m checking something, or I’ll pace a little bit and try to make it seem like I’m being productive. But honestly, it’s more that I feel like I can’t just stay seated through the whole thing. I’m aware of it while it’s happening, which makes it a little embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you also feel restless or find yourself getting up in other situations where you’re expected to stay seated, like during meals with family or while watching a movie at home?\n\nPatient: I mean… the clearest example is still work, honestly. The long sales strategy meetings are where it really stands out to me, because I know I’m supposed to stay seated and just be present, and instead I’ll start finding these little excuses to get up.\n\nLike I’ll refill my water when I don’t actually need water, or I’ll walk over by the printer and sort of hover there like I’m checking on something, or I’ll pace a little bit and try to make it look normal. It’s not like I’m storming out of the room or anything, but I do feel like I can’t just stay in the chair the whole time.\n\nWith things like meals or movies, I’m not sure I’ve paid as much attention to it. The work meetings are what made me think, “Okay, this is noticeable enough that maybe I should talk to someone.”\n\nDoctor: Outside of work meetings, do you ever feel restless or have the urge to get up during other situations like meals with friends or family, or while watching a movie at home?\n\nPatient: Honestly, outside of work I’m not as sure. I don’t want to say it never happens, but the thing that really stands out—and the reason I came in—is the work meetings. Those long sales strategy meetings are where I notice it the most because there’s this clear expectation that I’m supposed to stay seated and focused, and I just keep finding reasons not to be in the chair.\n\nLike I’ll get up to refill my water when I don’t really need any, or I’ll walk over toward the printer and kind of stand there like I’m checking on something, or I’ll pace for a minute and try to make it look like it has a purpose. It’s not like I’m trying to be disruptive. I just get this feeling like I can’t sit through the whole thing without moving. With meals or movies, I guess I haven’t paid as close attention, but the meetings are definitely the situation where it’s become obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any times outside of work—like when you’re at home watching TV or having a meal with friends or family—when you’ve felt restless or had the urge to get up and move around?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think, and honestly I don’t have a great outside-of-work example that comes to mind. Like, I don’t want to say it never happens at home or at meals, because maybe it does in smaller ways and I just haven’t been tracking it, but what really made me come in is the meeting thing.\n\nIt’s those long sales strategy meetings where I know I’m supposed to stay seated, and I’ll still keep finding these little reasons to get up. I’ll refill my water even if I barely drank any, or I’ll go over near the printer and kind of pace or hover there like I’m doing something useful. It’s not huge or dramatic, but I notice this urge to get out of the chair, and it feels awkward because I know everyone else is just sitting there.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall any specific times outside of work—like when you’re at home watching TV or sitting down for a meal with friends or family—when you’ve felt that same kind of restlessness or urge to get up and move around?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly still having trouble coming up with a clear example outside of work. Like, I can imagine maybe I shift around or get up during TV or something, but I don’t have a specific moment where I’m like, “Yes, that was the same thing.”\n\nThe one I keep coming back to is the long sales strategy meetings. That’s really what made me think I should come in, because I know I’m expected to just sit there and stay in the meeting, and I keep finding reasons to get up anyway. I’ll refill my water even if I don’t really need to, or I’ll walk over by the printer and kind of pace or stand there like I’m checking on something. It’s not dramatic, but it feels like I can’t just stay seated the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt restless or had the urge to move while sitting at home watching TV, eating with friends or family, or in any other non-work setting?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking specifically about home or meals or things like that, and I’m still not coming up with a really solid example there. It feels like this internal pressure to move, but I try to make it look normal because I don’t want people to think I’m being rude or not paying attention.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any time at home or during a social activity where you felt a strong urge to move or couldn't sit still, even if just for a short while?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking about home or social stuff, and I feel like I’m not giving you a great answer there because I can’t pull up one clear example like, “Oh, at dinner last Friday I couldn’t sit still.” It’s more that the strongest, most obvious version of it has been at work.\n\nWhat made me come in, honestly, is that during those long sales strategy meetings, I keep getting up even though I know I’m supposed to stay seated. I’m still not really coming up with a clear home example, honestly. Like, when you say relaxing at home, I don’t immediately think, “Yes, I’m always uncomfortable sitting on the couch,” or anything that obvious.

Like, I can imagine maybe I shift around or get up during TV or something, but I don’t have a specific moment where I’m like, “Yes, that was the same thing.”\n\nThe one I keep coming back to is the long sales strategy meetings. It feels like this internal pressure to move, but I try to make it look normal because I don’t want people to think I’m being rude or not paying attention.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any time at home or during a social activity where you felt a strong urge to move or couldn't sit still, even if just for a short while?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking about home or social stuff, and I feel like I’m not giving you a great answer there because I can’t pull up one clear example like, “Oh, at dinner last Friday I couldn’t sit still.” It’s more that the strongest, most obvious version of it has been at work.\n\nWhat made me come in, honestly, is that during those long sales strategy meetings, I keep getting up even though I know I’m supposed to stay seated. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I know I’m kind of circling the same answer, but honestly, at home or in relaxed situations I don’t have a specific example that jumps out at me. Like I can’t honestly say, “Yes, every time I’m watching TV I feel like I have to get up,” because that doesn’t feel quite accurate.

The thing that really made me notice it, and made me think I should talk to someone, is that I keep getting up during long sales strategy meetings at work when I know I’m supposed to stay seated. Even if it’s just something small, like getting more water when I don’t really need it, or walking over by the printer and kind of pacing there like I’m checking on something. It’s this uncomfortable feeling of needing to leave the chair, and I try to make it look normal because I’m aware it probably seems odd if I keep popping up for no reason.

I’m not sure I’d describe myself as someone who’s generally worried all the time for no reason. I mean, I do worry about normal things, like work stuff or whether I handled something the right way, but I don’t know that it feels like anxiety that comes out of nowhere.

Those long sales strategy meetings are where I notice it the most because there’s this clear expectation that I’m supposed to stay seated and focused, and I just keep finding reasons not to be in the chair.\n\nLike I’ll get up to refill my water when I don’t really need any, or I’ll walk over toward the printer and kind of stand there like I’m checking on something, or I’ll pace for a minute and try to make it look like it has a purpose. It’s not like I’m trying to be disruptive. I just get this feeling like I can’t sit through the whole thing without moving. With meals or movies, I guess I haven’t paid as close attention, but the meetings are definitely the situation where it’s become obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any times outside of work—like when you’re at home watching TV or having a meal with friends or family—when you’ve felt restless or had the urge to get up and move around?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think, and honestly I don’t have a great outside-of-work example that comes to mind. Like, I don’t want to say it never happens at home or at meals, because maybe it does in smaller ways and I just haven’t been tracking it, but what really made me come in is the meeting thing.\n\nIt’s those long sales strategy meetings where I know I’m supposed to stay seated, and I’ll still keep finding these little reasons to get up. Like I can’t honestly say, “Yes, every time I’m watching TV I feel like I have to get up,” because that doesn’t feel quite accurate.\n\nThe thing that really made me notice it, and made me think I should talk to someone, is that I keep getting up during long sales strategy meetings at work when I know I’m supposed to stay seated. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I know you’re asking specifically about home or relaxed situations, and I’m trying not to overstate it. I don’t have a really clear home example that I can point to, like “every time I watch TV I have to stand up.” It’s more that the clearest version of it, the thing that honestly made me come in, is at work.

During long sales strategy meetings, when I know I’m supposed to just stay seated, I keep finding reasons to get up. Like I’ll refill my water even if I barely drank any, or I’ll walk over near the printer and sort of pace there like I’m checking on something. It feels subtle in the moment, but I’m aware I’m basically trying to leave the chair, and I try to make it look normal. So at home, I’m not totally sure, but in that kind of sitting-still situation, yes, it’s definitely happening.

It feels like this internal pressure to move, but I try to make it look normal because I don’t want people to think I’m being rude or not paying attention.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any time at home or during a social activity where you felt a strong urge to move or couldn't sit still, even if just for a short while?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking about home or social stuff, and I feel like I’m not giving you a great answer there because I can’t pull up one clear example like, “Oh, at dinner last Friday I couldn’t sit still.” It’s more that the strongest, most obvious version of it has been at work.\n\nWhat made me come in, honestly, is that during those long sales strategy meetings, I keep getting up even though I know I’m supposed to stay seated.
